The Higher Structure of Symmetries of Axion-Maxwell Theory
Authors: Michele Del Zotto, Matteo Dell’Acqua, Elias Riedel Gårding
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
Generalized symmetries of quantum field theories can be characterized by topological defects/operators organized into a higher category. In this paper we consider the Axion-Maxwell field theory in four dimensions and, building on the construction of its topological defects by Choi, Lam, Shao, Hidaka, Nitta and Yokokura, we discuss field theoretical methods to compute some aspects of the higher structure of such category. In particular, we determine explicitly the generalized F-symbols for the non-invertible electric 1-form symmetry of the theory. Along the way, we clarify various aspects of the bottom-up worldvolume approach towards the calculus of defects.

Introduction

Global symmetries play a central role in the study of physical theories. In recent years a generalization of the concept of symmetry has been introduced [1] (see [2–10] for reviews), establishing the equivalence of symmetries with topological operators and defects of general (co)dimension. In particular, these new symmetries need not follow a group-like composition law, but may be non-invertible [11–59]. Over the last decade, generalized symmetries have found diverse applications in a wide range of quantum field theories [60–97, 97–157]. A new feature of these generalized symmetries is that topological defects can form junctions and networks of various codimension that give the symmetry a higher structure. A central question is how to compute the latter for a given field theory. The main aim of this paper is to perform a first step in computing the higher structure of Axion-Maxwell theory in four-dimensions, building on seminal work by Choi, Lam, Shao and Hidaka, Nitta, Yokokura [73, 156, 158–163] and generalizing previous results about massless QED in four-dimensions [48].

What is the mathematical structure encoding all the physical information present in the topological sector of the (extended) operators of a given QFT \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T? It is believed the latter is encoded in a higher tensor category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C (often called the symmetry category).1 A crucial aspect of the symmetry category is that topological defects can have junctions and intricate topological interfaces corresponding to higher morphisms. For a review, we refer our readers to Section 2 of [48]. Not only the topological defects, but also these topological interfaces can have a non-trivial action on the non-topological defects of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}$$\end{document}T. Many known properties of symmetries, such as ’t Hooft anomalies or fractionalization of various kinds, are encoded in the higher structure of the symmetry category.2 In particular, the whole higher structure is conserved under duality and symmetry-preserving renormalisation group (RG) flows. This is a key motivation to develop techniques to compute the higher structure of symmetries.

Currently, there are two main approaches to generalized symmetries, which we call the quiche (or SymTFT) approach [4, 89, 100, 106, 115, 165–174] and the worldvolume approach [20, 158, 160–162, 175–196]. The quiche approach relies on the existence of an isomorphism of the field theory of interest with a bulk/boundary system consisting of a TQFT in one higher dimension on a slab with two boundaries, one topological the other a relative theory. The pair of the bulk TQFT and the topological boundary is the quiche, and it encodes all the features of the topological defects of the theory of interest in the well-developed calculus of topological defects in TQFTs. However, this construction relies on the existence of such an isomorphism, which for the most general symmetries is not yet completely understood.3 Oftentimes, such an isomorphism is not known and one needs to work directly with the QFT of interest to compute the higher structure of its symmetries. This is the context of the worldvolume approach, where the topological defects are constructed using field theoretical techniques without reference to an isomorphism with a higher dimensional system. This paper is in the context of the worldvolume approach to the higher structure. Our focus is to study the higher structure of the symmetries of the 4d Axion-Maxwell theory, whose rich symmetry structure makes it a fruitful setting for case studies of generalized symmetries [72, 85, 156, 190, 191, 197, 198].

Of particular interest are the higher associators, topological defects encoding the associativity property of the fusion product. For 2d theories, the topological codimension one defects are lines, the fusion interfaces are points and the associators (of three topological lines) are numbers (the so-called F-symbols) satisfying a pentagon identity (which follows from requesting the compatibility of associativity with the fusion of four objects). For higher dimensional field theories, topological codimension (p+1)(p+1) defects, have fusion interfaces of codimension (p+2)(p+2), and corresponding associators of n objects of codimension p+np+n. Higher associators and obstruction to gauging (’t Hooft anomalies) are deeply interconnected [51–53, 76, 199].

A first step towards determining the higher structure of chiral symmetry of (3+1)d(3+1)d massless QED have been obtained in [48]. Among other results, that paper determined the fusion rules, fusion interfaces and F-symbols of the symmetry category, which are 2d TQFTs. The main result of this paper is an analogous analysis for the symmetry of (3+1)d(3+1)d Axion-Maxwell theory. In particular we determine the fusion rules and interfaces as well as the F-symbol 1d TQFTs controlling the associativity of the non-invertible electric 1-form symmetry of this model. We show that these have a non-trivial action on extended operators of the theory and that these satisfy stringent consistency conditions. The symmetry category of Axion-Maxwell theory is richer than that of massless QED. For the 1-form symmetry, the resulting TQFT coefficients in particular have decomposition (see e.g. [200]). Moreover, the 1d F-symbol TQFTs have more symmetries than the ones enjoyed by the 2d F-symbol TQFTs of the 0-form symmetries, which leads to a collection of identities that they have to satisfy.4

This work is organized as follows. In Sects. 2 to 5 we review the previously known data of the symmetry category and charges of (3+1)d(3+1)d Axion Electrodynamics. In Sect. 6 we derive the fusion rules and interfaces of the non-invertible electric 1-form symmetry. Their associator is described in Sect. 7, where we also check the consistency and analyze the consequences of our results with other previously known structures of the theory.

In order to make the work self-contained, we review some mathematical results on the anomalies of invertible symmetries and on generalized gauging in 2 and 3 dimensions respectively in sections A and B. Finally, in section C we review the notion of minimal theories and we presented an alternative derivation of the fusion rules of chiral defects in QED, starting from a Lagrangian description.

Genuine Topological Defects

To fix notations and conventions let us recall that the Euclidean action of the Axion-Maxwell theory is given by:1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S = \frac{f^2}{2}\int \text {d}\theta \wedge *\text {d}\theta +\frac{1}{2e^2}\int F\wedge *F -i\frac{K}{8\pi ^2}\int \theta F\wedge F, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S=f22∫dθ∧∗dθ+12e2∫F∧∗F-iK8π2∫θF∧F,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \text {d}{A}$$\end{document}F=dA is the field strength for a 1-form U(1) gauge field and θ θ a 0-form gauge field, i.e. a compact scalar5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \sim \theta + 2\pi $$\end{document}θ∼θ+2π. The two constants f and e are respectively the axion decay constant electric charge and they are unconstrained, while the axion–photon coupling K is an integer (on spin manifolds) in order for the interaction term to be gauge invariant. Neither A nor θ θ are gauge invariant, however we can define gauge invariant operators either by considering their field strengths, i.e. F and dθ dθ together with their Hodge duals, or by considering their holonomies. Hence, the gauge-invariant operators of the theory are given by Wilson lines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_q = e^{2\pi iq \int _\gamma A}$$\end{document}Wq=e2πiq∫γA and ’t Hooft lines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m$$\end{document}Hm, as well as the axion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _n = e^{in\theta }$$\end{document}Θn=einθ and the string worldsheet \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_w$$\end{document}Sw, analogue of the ’t Hooft line for θ θ.

Due to the axion-photon coupling, the definition of ’t Hooft lines and string worldsheets differs slightly from the naive one in terms of boundary conditions for the gauge fields A and ϕ ϕ. We will expand on this remark in Sect. 4.

Let us recall what happens at trivial coupling K = 0K=0. With this choice of coupling, the theory describes two non-interacting free gauge theories: a free Maxwell subtheory with a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(1)}_e \times U(1)^{(1)}_m$$\end{document}U(1)e(1)×U(1)m(1) 1-form symmetry (with mixed anomaly) computing the electric and magnetic charge of dyonic lines, and the theory of a free compact boson with a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}_s$$\end{document}U(1)s(0) symmetry which acts by shifting the axion field and a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(2)}_w$$\end{document}U(1)w(2) measuring the winding number:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \theta \rightarrow \theta + 2\pi \Lambda \quad \quad w = \oint _\gamma \frac{\text {d}\theta }{2\pi } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ→θ+2πΛw=∮γdθ2πagain with a mixed anomaly. Let us note that both these symmetries are non-linearly realised and thus they are spontaneously broken, we can therefore interpret the massless degrees of freedom, i.e. the photon and the axion, as Goldstone bosons [1].

We start the analysis of the symmetry category by looking at the genuine defects, i.e. (higher) endomorphisms of the identity.

To see the higher-group structure of the theory we can (minimally) couple it to a background for all the previously described symmetries [191, 197]:8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} S&= \frac{f^2}{2}\int _X (\text {d}\theta - 2\pi B_s^{(1)}) \wedge *(\text {d}\theta -2\pi B_s^{(1)})\\&\quad + \frac{1}{2e^2}\int _X(F - 2\pi B_e^{(2)})\wedge *(F - 2\pi B_e^{(2)}) \\&\quad + i\int _X\theta G_w^{(4)} - i\int _X H_m^{(3)}\wedge A \\&\quad - \dfrac{iK}{8\pi ^2}\int _Y (\text {d}\theta - 2\pi B_s^{(1)}) \wedge (F - 2\pi B_e^{(2)}) \wedge (F - 2\pi B_e^{(2)}), \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S=f22∫X(dθ-2πBs(1))∧∗(dθ-2πBs(1))+12e2∫X(F-2πBe(2))∧∗(F-2πBe(2))+i∫XθGw(4)-i∫XHm(3)∧A-iK8π2∫Y(dθ-2πBs(1))∧(F-2πBe(2))∧(F-2πBe(2)),where Y is a 5-dimensional open manifold that bounds the spacetime X and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_s^{(1)}$$\end{document}Bs(1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_e^{(2)}$$\end{document}Be(2) are background gauge fields quantized so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\oint B_s^{(1)}, \oint B_e^{(2)} \in \frac{1}{K}\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}∮Bs(1),∮Be(2)∈1KZ. As anticipated, the nonlinear terms in the definition are the hallmark of a nontrivial higher-group structure of the symmetry category. We will explore the consequences of Eq. (9) in Sect. 4.

One may now check that Eq. (8) is invariant under modified gauge transformations of the form:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\theta \longmapsto \theta + 2\pi \Lambda _s^{(0)}, \quad B_s^{(1)} \longmapsto B_s^{(1)} + \text {d}\Lambda _s^{(0)}, \\&A \longmapsto A + 2\pi \Lambda _e^{(0)} \quad B_e^{(2)} \longmapsto B_e^{(2)} + \text {d}\Lambda _e^{(1)}, \\&B_w^{(3)} \longmapsto B_w^{(3)} + \text {d}\Lambda _w^{(2)} - K {(}B_{e}^{(2)} \wedge \Lambda _{e}^{(1)} + \frac{1}{2}\Lambda _{e}^{(1)} \wedge \text {d}\Lambda _{e}^{(1)}) , \\&B_{m}^{(2)} \longmapsto B_{m}^{(2)} + \text {d}\Lambda _{m}^{(1)} - K {(}\Lambda _s^{(0)} B_{e}^{(2)} - B_s^{(1)} \wedge \Lambda _{e}^{(1)} - \text {d}\Lambda _s^{(0)} \wedge \Lambda _{e}^{(1)}). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}θ⟼θ+2πΛs(0),Bs(1)⟼Bs(1)+dΛs(0),A⟼A+2πΛe(0)Be(2)⟼Be(2)+dΛe(1),Bw(3)⟼Bw(3)+dΛw(2)-K(Be(2)∧Λe(1)+12Λe(1)∧dΛe(1)),Bm(2)⟼Bm(2)+dΛm(1)-K(Λs(0)Be(2)-Bs(1)∧Λe(1)-dΛs(0)∧Λe(1)).The mixed gauge transformations of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{m}^{(2)}$$\end{document}Bm(2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_w^{(3)}$$\end{document}Bw(3) are another sign of the higher group structure.

The inflow action can be derived to be [197]:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_{\text {inflow}} = 2\pi i \int _Y {(} G_w^{(4)} \wedge B_s^{(1)} + H_m^{(3)} \wedge B_e^{(2)}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sinflow=2πi∫Y(Gw(4)∧Bs(1)+Hm(3)∧Be(2)),which can be interpreted as the improved version of the mixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}_s \times U(1)^{(2)}_w$$\end{document}U(1)s(0)×U(1)w(2) anomaly of the free compact boson and mixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)_e^{(1)}\times U(1)^{(1)}_m$$\end{document}U(1)e(1)×U(1)m(1) anomaly of the free photon at K = 0K=0.

Finally, one could also consider a further conserved current given by the Chern–Weil term [189, 203]12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} j_{CW}^{(3)} = j_w^{(1)} \wedge j_m^{(2)} = \frac{d\theta }{2\pi } \wedge \frac{F}{2\pi }; \quad \text {d}{j_{CW}^{(3)}} = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}jCW(3)=jw(1)∧jm(2)=dθ2π∧F2π;djCW(3)=0,and define the topological operators:13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta _{\alpha }^{(CW)}(\Sigma ^{(3)}) \equiv \exp \left( 2\pi i\alpha \oint _{\Sigma ^{(3)}} j_{CW}^{(3)} \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ηα(CW)(Σ(3))≡exp2πiα∮Σ(3)jCW(3).However, these defects are trivial. Indeed, the equation of motion for the electric current (4) implies that, given a 3-dimensional closed manifold we have14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \exp \left( 2\pi i\alpha \int _{\Sigma ^{(3)}}\frac{\text {d}{\theta }}{2\pi }\wedge \frac{F}{2\pi }\right) = \exp \left( \frac{2\pi }{e^2} \frac{\alpha }{K}\oint _{\Sigma ^{(3)}} \text {d}*F\right) = 1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}exp2πiα∫Σ(3)dθ2π∧F2π=exp2πe2αK∮Σ(3)d∗F=1,using Stokes’ theorem. Another, more formal, way to rephrase this fact is that there exists an invertible morphism6:15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta _{\alpha }^{(e)}\in \operatorname {Hom}_{\Omega \mathcal {C}}(\mathbb {1}^{(0)}; \eta ^{(CW)}_\alpha ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ηα(e)∈HomΩC(1(0);ηα(CW)).

Condensation defects

Given the invertible symmetry operators, a further class of topological operators is given by condensation defects, obtained by higher gauging of discrete subgroups [204, 205]; However, a simultaneous condensation of a subgroup of both symmetries (without mixing discrete torsion) factorizes as two independent condensations of the two projections:18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}_{N, M, \alpha ,0}^{(0,m, w)}=\mathcal {C}_{N,\alpha }^{(0,m)}\mathcal {C}_{M}^{(0,w)},\quad \quad \mathcal {C}_{N,M}^{(1,m,w)}=\mathcal {C}_{N}^{(1,m)}\mathcal {C}_{M}^{(1,w)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CN,M,α,0(0,m,w)=CN,α(0,m)CM(0,w),CN,M(1,m,w)=CN(1,m)CM(1,w),since the winding and magnetic symmetry don’t interact with each other (i.e. their subcategory is that of a trivial 2-group) even at a nontrivial coupling. Meanwhile, the other condensates do not factorize in the same way, because the electric and shift symmetries interact in a nontrivial way, as we will explain in more detail later.

On top of that, in [72] it was shown that the axion–Maxwell theory is self-dual under 1-gauging of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_N^{(1)} \times \mathbb {Z}_N^{(2)} \subset U(1)^{(1)}_m \times U(1)^{(2)}_w$$\end{document}ZN(1)×ZN(2)⊂U(1)m(1)×U(1)w(2) subgroup with any choice of β β, in the sense that19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {C}_{N,N,0,\beta }^{(0,m,w)} \sim \mathbb {1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CN,N,0,β(0,m,w)∼1.This means that there exists an invertible topological interface between it and the transparent defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {1}$$\end{document}1, or that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}_{N,N,0,\beta }^{(0,m,w)}(\Sigma ^{(3)})$$\end{document}CN,N,0,β(0,m,w)(Σ(3)) is trivial when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma ^{(3)}$$\end{document}Σ(3) is closed.

Noninvertible defects

We exhausted the clearly conserved currents of the theory. However, there are cases in which it is still possible to define topological operators starting from non-conserved currents. From now on, we will work with K=1K=1 (unless explicitly specified otherwise).

The conservation (4) of the shift current is spoiled by a term analogous to that of the chiral current in massless QED: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {d}{j_s} = \frac{1}{2} j_m^{(2)} \wedge j_m^{(2)}$$\end{document}djs=12jm(2)∧jm(2). We can therefore define the noninvertible electric defect as:24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N} \equiv \eta ^{(e)}_{p/N} \otimes \mathcal {A}_2^{N,p} {[}j_w^{(1)}; j_m^{(2)}] = \exp \bigg (\frac{2\pi }{e^2} \frac{p}{N} \oint *F\bigg ) \mathcal {A}_2^{N,p} {[}j_w^{(1)},j_m^{(2)}]; \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e)≡ηp/N(e)⊗A2N,p[jw(1);jm(2)]=exp(2πe2pN∮∗F)A2N,p[jw(1),jm(2)];it is topological because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\pi i \frac{p}{N} \int _{X_3} \text {d}{j_e^{(2)}} = -S_\text {inflow}{[}j_w^{(1)}, j_m^{(2)}]$$\end{document}2πipN∫X3dje(2)=-Sinflow[jw(1),jm(2)]; the electric symmetry defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}ηp/N(e) cancels the anomaly of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_2^{N,p}$$\end{document}A2N,p.

Finally, there are other arguments to arrive at the same definitions of noninvertible shift and electric defects. For example, both can be seen as half (higher) duality defects [72]; we will make use of this in Sect. 5.

All the defects defined so far are built from the corresponding naive defect of the trivially coupled theory up to factors involving currents for other symmetries. Thus, their original action is still present:25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \eta _\alpha ^{(m)}:H_m \mapsto \exp (2\pi i\alpha m)H_m, \quad&\eta _\alpha ^{(w)}:S_w \mapsto \exp (2\pi i\alpha w)S_w,\\ \mathcal {D}_{p/N}^{(s)}:\exp (i\theta ) \mapsto \exp \bigg (\frac{2\pi ip}{N}\bigg )\exp (i\theta ), \quad&\mathcal {D}_{p/N}^{(e)}:W_q \mapsto \exp \bigg (\frac{2\pi ipq}{N}\bigg )W_q. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ηα(m):Hm↦exp(2πiαm)Hm,ηα(w):Sw↦exp(2πiαw)Sw,Dp/N(s):exp(iθ)↦exp(2πipN)exp(iθ),Dp/N(e):Wq↦exp(2πipqN)Wq.Obviously, the non-invertible defects have additional non-invertible action. In order to derive them, we first need to explore their topological junctions, as we will do in the following paragraph.

Topological Junctions

Invertible symmetries

The invertible magnetic and winding symmetries enjoy no nontrivial higher morphisms. More formally:26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \textrm{Hom}_{\Omega ^2\mathcal {C}}(\eta _\alpha ^{(m)} \otimes \eta _\beta ^{(m)}; \eta _\gamma ^{(m)}) = \boldsymbol{\delta }_{\alpha +\beta }^{\gamma } L_{\alpha ,\beta }^{\gamma } ,\quad&L_{\alpha ,\beta }^{\gamma } \simeq \textrm{Vec}, \\ \textrm{Hom}_{\Omega ^3\mathcal {C}}(\eta _\alpha ^{(w)} \otimes \eta _\beta ^{(w)}; \eta _\gamma ^{(w)}) = \boldsymbol{\delta }_{\alpha +\beta }^{\gamma } m_{\alpha ,\beta }^{\gamma } ,\quad&m_{\alpha ,\beta }^{\gamma } \simeq \mathbb {C}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}HomΩ2C(ηα(m)⊗ηβ(m);ηγ(m))=δα+βγLα,βγ,Lα,βγ≃Vec,HomΩ3C(ηα(w)⊗ηβ(w);ηγ(w))=δα+βγmα,βγ,mα,βγ≃C.This means that there is no interface between defects labeled by different group elements; meanwhile, there is always the freedom to dress a submanifold of the defect with a decoupled TQFT of the right dimension.

Noninvertible symmetries

The definitions of both classes of noninvertible defects involve a minimal theory coupled to a current for a bulk symmetry. As we discussed in the previous chapter, this allows for symmetry defects of the bulk symmetry to end topologically on the defects of the minimal theory. More precisely, the defects of the coupled minimal theory will be topological only if attached to a bulk defect of one higher dimension supported on a bounding hypersurface.Fig. 1Topological junctions between invertible and noninvertible defects

Topological junctions between invertible and noninvertible defects

(Noninvertible) higher group

This theory exhibits a (noninvertible generalization of a) higher group structure, which is characterized by a nontrivial interplay between the various layers of the symmetry category.

For simplicity, let’s first discuss the invertible version of this structure, working at a coupling level |K| > 1|K|>1 following [72, appendix A]. We remind the reader that a network of defects for a continuous (higher form) symmetry is equivalent to (the Pontryagin dual of the holonomies of) a flat background. If we impose the flatness conditions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G^{(4)} = 0$$\end{document}G(4)=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{(3)} = 0$$\end{document}H(3)=0, Eq. (9) takes the form:39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \text {d}{B_w^{(3)}} = -\frac{K}{2} B_{e}^{(2)} \wedge B_{e}^{(2)}, \quad \text {d}{B_m^{(2)}} = -K B_s^{(1)} \wedge B_{e}^{(2)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}dBw(3)=-K2Be(2)∧Be(2),dBm(2)=-KBs(1)∧Be(2),which implies the following constraints on the allowed defect configurations:The one-dimensional intersection of an electric defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\eta }^{(e)}_j$$\end{document}η~j(e) and a shift defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\eta }^{(s)}_k$$\end{document}η~k(s) sources a magnetic defect. This emission is what makes the junction topological. Non-topological Operators and their Junctions

The definition of ’t Hooft lines and axion strings suffers from some subtleties in the presence of a non-trivial coupling K ≠ 0K≠0. As a result, the spectrum of the theory is enriched by both topological and non-topological junctions. A full account is given in [72, section 5]; what follows is a quick summary.Fig. 3Junctions involving non-topological operators: ’t Hooft lines host endpoints of Wilson lines (for compatible charges) and winding defects (3a) and they link nontrivially; axion strings host endpoints of Wilson lines (for compatible charges) and magnetic defects (3b) and they link nontrivially

Junctions involving non-topological operators: ’t Hooft lines host endpoints of Wilson lines (for compatible charges) and winding defects (3a) and they link nontrivially; axion strings host endpoints of Wilson lines (for compatible charges) and magnetic defects (3b) and they link nontrivially

’t Hooft lines The naive definition of an ’t Hooft line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m(\gamma )$$\end{document}Hm(γ), imposing a boundary condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d\left( dA\right) =2\pi m\delta (\gamma )$$\end{document}ddA=2πmδ(γ), is not gauge invariant in the presence of a nontrivial coupling K. Under a gauge transformation of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \rightarrow \theta + 2\pi $$\end{document}θ→θ+2π the naive ’t Hooft line gets dressed by a Wilson line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{Km}$$\end{document}WKm via the Witten effect. Formally, the wordline theory suffers from a gauge anomaly, and we can obtain a well-defined ’t Hooft line by adding local degrees of freedom that cancel it. Whichever choice of theory is made, it must have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(-1)}$$\end{document}U(1)(-1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}$$\end{document}U(1)(0) symmetries coupled to the bulk winding and magnetic symmetries such that the bulk defects end topologically on the worldline defects. As we will show later, these topological junctions are needed in order to coherently implement the noninvertible electric and shift actions.

Moreover, the quantum mechanical theory on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m$$\end{document}Hm hosts non-topological points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_j$$\end{document}Oj with charge j under the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}$$\end{document}U(1)(0) symmetry. Due to the coupling of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}$$\end{document}U(1)(0) symmetry to the bulk winding symmetry, and the noninvertible action of the electric defects, one can show that these points acquire a bulk electric charge jm (see Fig. 3a, as well as the discussion on the non-invertible symmetry action in the next chapter) and one can thus coherently end \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{jm}$$\end{document}Wjm on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}_j$$\end{document}Oj defects of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m$$\end{document}Hm.

Axion strings Analogously, string worldsheets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_w$$\end{document}Sw enjoy a nontrivial worldsheet action which allows both magnetic defects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_\alpha $$\end{document}ηα(m) (for all α α) and Wilson lines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_q$$\end{document}Wq (if and only if q≡ 0q≡0 mod w) to end on it. The endpoints of the magnetic defect act as symmetry defects for a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}$$\end{document}U(1)(0) symmetry on the string worldsheet, whose charged objects are exactly the endpoints of the Wilson lines.

In fact, due to the coupling of the localized \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U(1)^{(0)}$$\end{document}U(1)(0) symmetry to the bulk winding symmetry, and the noninvertible action of the electric defects, one can show that these nontopological points acquire a bulk electric charge wj (see Fig. 3b, as well as the discussion on the non-invertible symmetry action in the next chapter) and one can therefore coherently end \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_jw$$\end{document}Wjw on the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathcal {O}}_j$$\end{document}O¯j defects of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_w$$\end{document}Sw.

Noninvertible Actions

Let us now discuss the non-invertible part of the action of the electric and shift symmetries. In order to derive these actions, we will extensively use results from the previous chapters.

Shift symmetry First of all, we use theorem C.2 to derive a construction of the shift defect as a result of half space gauging [158]:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p}{N}}[\partial X^{(4)}]&=\int [\mathcal {D}\Phi ][\mathcal {D}b][\mathcal {D}c]\exp \left( S_{\text {axion}}+\frac{2\pi ip}{N}\int _{\partial X^{(4)}} j^{(3)}_s\right) \\&\quad \times \exp \left( \int _{X^{(4)}}\frac{iN}{2\pi }b\wedge dc+\frac{iN(p)^{-1}_N}{4\pi }b\wedge b+\frac{i}{2\pi }b\wedge F\right) , \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DpN(s)[∂X(4)]=∫[DΦ][Db][Dc]expSaxion+2πipN∫∂X(4)js(3)×exp∫X(4)iN2πb∧dc+iN(p)N-14πb∧b+i2πb∧F,where we observe that Axion Maxwell is self-dual under the higher gauging of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_N^{(m)}$$\end{document}ZN(m) with discrete torsion p in the same way massless QED is (once again, the differences between the two theories do not involve the magnetic symmetry). One could have arrived at the same result by noting that the shift defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}_{p/N}^{(s)}$$\end{document}Dp/N(s) implements the transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta \rightarrow \theta + \frac{2\pi i p}{N}$$\end{document}θ→θ+2πipN, which provide electric charge to magnetically charged lines via the Witten effect. Alternatively, using the half-gauge construction, we can argue that the ’t Hooft line picks up a phase \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\exp {(}\frac{2\pi i k}{N})$$\end{document}exp(2πikN) when it crosses a component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{\frac{k}{N}}$$\end{document}ηkN(m) of the network of defects implementing the magnetic gauge, while the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{\frac{p}{N}}$$\end{document}ηpN(m) factor picks up the opposite contribution due to the discrete torsion, making the combination well-defined even in the presence of the network. Transforming genuine operator into non-genuine ones is a hallmark of non-invertibility.

Fusion Interfaces

In this section, we review the fusion interfaces derived in [48] between noninvertible chiral defects of massless QED, adapting the argument to our setup. We then derive analogous interfaces for the fusion of noninvertible electric defects of the axion Maxwell theory.

We will abundantly use the following notation (borrowed by [48]): Given two defects labeled by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{p_{1,2}}{N_{1,2}}$$\end{document}p1,2N1,2 we define47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&M= \gcd (N_1, N_2), \quad & L= {{\,\textrm{lcm}\,}}(N_1, N_2), \quad & K_i= \frac{N_i}{M}\\&M'=\gcd (p_1K_2+p_2K_1, L), \quad & p_3=\frac{p_1K_2+p_2K_1}{M'}, \quad & N_3=\frac{L}{M'}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M=gcd(N1,N2),L=lcm(N1,N2),Ki=NiMM′=gcd(p1K2+p2K1,L),p3=p1K2+p2K1M′,N3=LM′.We will denote with the symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\cdot )^{-1}_N$$\end{document}(·)N-1 the multiplicative inverse of an element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_N^\times $$\end{document}ZN×. Moreover, we will use the modern terminology of gaugeable data as algebra objects (see section B).

Noninvertible 0-form symmetry

Once again, due to their analogous definition, the fusion of the shift symmetry is analogous to that of chiral defects in massless QED computed in [48]. In particular, the fusion coefficients are not merely integers, but TQFTs describing additional degrees of freedom, confined to the fusion interface but coupled to the bulk theory.

Prop 2

Consider the fusion of the type: 52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{1}{4}}\otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{1}{4}} \rightarrow \mathcal {C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{1}{4};\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{1}{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D14(s)⊗D14(s)→C14;1412D12(s), where we denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{1}{4};\frac{1}{4}}$$\end{document}C14;1412 the generic TFT coefficient. However, there is no such junction between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{1/4}$$\end{document}η1/4(m) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{1/2}$$\end{document}D1/2(s). Thus, the endlines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{1,2}$$\end{document}L1,2 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{1/4}$$\end{document}η1/4(m) cannot move across the interface: we will call these lines fractionally/incoherently coupled to the bulk. In the general case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_1}{N_1}} \otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_2}{N_2}}$$\end{document}Dp1N1(s)⊗Dp2N2(s) can host the endlines of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_L^{(m)}\subset U(1)^{(m)}$$\end{document}ZL(m)⊂U(1)(m) subgroup of the magnetic symmetries, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{p_3/N_3}$$\end{document}Dp3/N3(s) can only host the endlines of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{N_3}^{(m)} = \mathbb {Z}_{\frac{L}{M'}}^{(m)}\subset \mathbb {Z}_L^{(m)}$$\end{document}ZN3(m)=ZLM′(m)⊂ZL(m) subgroup. The (improperly coupled) lines not in the kernel of the map: 53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathbb {Z}_{N_1}\times \mathbb {Z}_{N_2}&\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}_{M'}\\ (s,t)&\mapsto (p_1K_1s+p_2K_2t)\ \text {mod}\ M' \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ZN1×ZN2→ZM′(s,t)↦(p1K1s+p2K2t)modM′ are stuck at the interface. Even more generally, by the same reasoning, the bulk coupling must match across the interface: 54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{s_1 p_1}{N_1} + \frac{s_2 p_2}{N_2} = \frac{s_3 p_3}{N_3} \pmod {1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}s1p1N1+s2p2N2=s3p3N3(mod1), and since there is only one line with a given coupling in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^{N_3,p_3}$$\end{document}AN3,p3, the action of the interfaces is fixed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _m$$\end{document}αm up to eventual freedom in the action of the interfaces is given by lines in the eventual decoupled coefficient.

Assuming such a channel exists, the action of the interface on the lines of the original \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_1}{N_1}}\otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_2}{N_2}}$$\end{document}Dp1N1(s)⊗Dp2N2(s) defect is highly constrained by the coupling with the bulk magnetic symmetry. This can be best illustrated by an example. Consider the fusion of the type: 52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{1}{4}}\otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{1}{4}} \rightarrow \mathcal {C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{1}{4};\frac{1}{4}}\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{1}{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D14(s)⊗D14(s)→C14;1412D12(s), where we denote with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\frac{1}{4};\frac{1}{4}}$$\end{document}C14;1412 the generic TFT coefficient. Before the interface, a bulk magnetic defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{1/4}$$\end{document}η1/4(m) can end on both of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{1/4}$$\end{document}D1/4(s). However, there is no such junction between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{1/4}$$\end{document}η1/4(m) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{1/2}$$\end{document}D1/2(s). Thus, the endlines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{1,2}$$\end{document}L1,2 of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{1/4}$$\end{document}η1/4(m) cannot move across the interface: we will call these lines fractionally/incoherently coupled to the bulk. In the general case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_1}{N_1}} \otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_2}{N_2}}$$\end{document}Dp1N1(s)⊗Dp2N2(s) can host the endlines of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_L^{(m)}\subset U(1)^{(m)}$$\end{document}ZL(m)⊂U(1)(m) subgroup of the magnetic symmetries, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{p_3/N_3}$$\end{document}Dp3/N3(s) can only host the endlines of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{N_3}^{(m)} = \mathbb {Z}_{\frac{L}{M'}}^{(m)}\subset \mathbb {Z}_L^{(m)}$$\end{document}ZN3(m)=ZLM′(m)⊂ZL(m) subgroup. The (improperly coupled) lines not in the kernel of the map: 53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathbb {Z}_{N_1}\times \mathbb {Z}_{N_2}&\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}_{M'}\\ (s,t)&\mapsto (p_1K_1s+p_2K_2t)\ \text {mod}\ M' \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ZN1×ZN2→ZM′(s,t)↦(p1K1s+p2K2t)modM′ are stuck at the interface. Even more generally, by the same reasoning, the bulk coupling must match across the interface: 54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{s_1 p_1}{N_1} + \frac{s_2 p_2}{N_2} = \frac{s_3 p_3}{N_3} \pmod {1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}s1p1N1+s2p2N2=s3p3N3(mod1), and since there is only one line with a given coupling in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^{N_3,p_3}$$\end{document}AN3,p3, the action of the interfaces is fixed by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _m$$\end{document}αm up to eventual freedom in the action of the interfaces is given by lines in the eventual decoupled coefficient.

Luckily, there is exactly one gaugeable subgroup and it selects exactly the desired lines.

Moreover, while in the gauged theory the charge of a line doesn’t completely characterize the line, one can identify a decomposition of the spectrum/theory in which the redundancy corresponds exactly to the presence of a decoupled TFT coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^{N_3,p_3}$$\end{document}AN3,p3.

Remark 1

In [158] the authors computed the specific case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{p_1}{N_1}=-\frac{p_2}{N_2}=\frac{1}{N}$$\end{document}p1N1=-p2N2=1N obtaining:55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_\frac{1}{N}\otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{-\frac{1}{N}} =\mathcal {C}^{(0,m)}_{N,0}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D1N(s)⊗D-1N(s)=CN,0(0,m).There is a morphism between the condensate and the trivial operator which can either be seen as the Dirichlet boundary condition or the gauging of the quantum symmetry living on the condensation itself, which thus matches our result:56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_\frac{1}{N}\otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{-\frac{1}{N}} =\mathcal {C}^{(0,m)}_{N,,0} \xrightarrow [\text {Dirichlet}]{\text {m}_{N}}\mathbb {1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D1N(s)⊗D-1N(s)=CN,,0(0,m)→DirichletmN1,

Example 1

Let’s explicitly compute the example:57We can define the lines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha $$\end{document}Lα and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\beta $$\end{document}Lβ as follows:58\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&L_\alpha =L_1L_2^5, & L_\alpha ^8=1, & h[L_\alpha ]=\frac{3}{4}, & \alpha _m(L_\alpha )=0,\\&L_\beta =L_1L_2, & L_\beta ^8=1, & h[L_\beta ]=\frac{1}{4}, & \alpha _m(L_\alpha )=\frac{1}{2}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lα=L1L25,Lα8=1,h[Lα]=34,αm(Lα)=0,Lβ=L1L2,Lβ8=1,h[Lβ]=14,αm(Lα)=12,and it is easy to check that they trivially braid with each other. We would like to say that they generate a subtheory of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^{8,12}\otimes \mathcal {A}^{8,4}[4 j_m^{(2)}]$$\end{document}A8,12⊗A8,4[4jm(2)], but this statement has two problems. On the one hand, both of the theories are ill-defined since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gcd (8,4)=4=\gcd (8,12)\ne 1$$\end{document}gcd(8,4)=4=gcd(8,12)≠1, i.e. the contains nontrivial lines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha ^{2j}$$\end{document}Lα2j and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\beta ^{2j}$$\end{document}Lβ2j undetectable by elements of the subtheories. The second problem is that they have a non-empty intersection given exactly by the invisible lines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha ^2=L_\beta ^2$$\end{document}Lα2=Lβ2.

Both problems can be solved by gauging the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_4^{(1)}$$\end{document}Z4(1) anomaly-free (spinless) decoupled subgroup generated by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2=L_\alpha ^2=L_\beta ^2$$\end{document}L12L22=Lα2=Lβ2.

Let’s look at the effect of the gauging on the lines discussed above:It identifies lines which differ by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha ^2$$\end{document}Lα2. In particular, we have: 59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_\alpha \leadsto [L_\alpha ],\quad [ L_\alpha ]^2=[\mathbb {1}], \quad \quad L_\beta \leadsto [L_\beta ],\quad [L_\beta ]^2=[\mathbb {1}], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lα⇝[Lα],[Lα]2=[1],Lβ⇝[Lβ],[Lβ]2=[1], where the quantum number of the orbits is well-defined and coincides with the ones of each of their representatives (since the gauged algebra is spinless and decoupled).It selects only lines which trivially braid with the generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_4^{(1)}$$\end{document}Z4(1) generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2$$\end{document}L12L22. However, we found \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2$$\end{document}L12L22 exactly by requiring this condition: 60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {(}L_1^2L_2^2)(\gamma _1)L_\alpha (\gamma _2)\rangle =0=\langle {(}L_1^2L_2^2)(\gamma _1)L_\beta (\gamma _2)\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨(L12L22)(γ1)Lα(γ2)⟩=0=⟨(L12L22)(γ1)Lβ(γ2)⟩, and thus, this step has no effect on the lines in question.It follows that now the lines actually generate a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^{2,1}\otimes \mathcal {A}^{2,1}[j_m^{(2)}]$$\end{document}A2,1⊗A2,1[jm(2)] subtheory. As a consequence of the minimality properties (theorem C.1) of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}^{N,p}$$\end{document}AN,p theories we have:61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{1/8}\otimes \mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{1/8}}{\mathbb {Z}_4^{(1)}}=\mathcal {A}^{2,1}\otimes \mathcal {A}^{2,1}[j_m^{(2)}]\otimes \mathcal {T}', \end{aligned}$$\end{document}D1/8(s)⊗D1/8(s)Z4(1)=A2,1⊗A2,1[jm(2)]⊗T′,for some unknown theory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}'$$\end{document}T′. Finally, we can conclude that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}'=\mathbb {1}$$\end{document}T′=1, by analyzing the effect of gauging on the remaining lines of the original theory. Indeed, we can write a generic line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} modulo \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}12:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} It identifies lines which differ by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha ^2$$\end{document}Lα2. It identifies lines which differ by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha ^2$$\end{document}Lα2. In particular, we have: 59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_\alpha \leadsto [L_\alpha ],\quad [ L_\alpha ]^2=[\mathbb {1}], \quad \quad L_\beta \leadsto [L_\beta ],\quad [L_\beta ]^2=[\mathbb {1}], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lα⇝[Lα],[Lα]2=[1],Lβ⇝[Lβ],[Lβ]2=[1], where the quantum number of the orbits is well-defined and coincides with the ones of each of their representatives (since the gauged algebra is spinless and decoupled).

It selects only lines which trivially braid with the generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_4^{(1)}$$\end{document}Z4(1) generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2$$\end{document}L12L22. However, we found \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2$$\end{document}L12L22 exactly by requiring this condition: 60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {(}L_1^2L_2^2)(\gamma _1)L_\alpha (\gamma _2)\rangle =0=\langle {(}L_1^2L_2^2)(\gamma _1)L_\beta (\gamma _2)\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨(L12L22)(γ1)Lα(γ2)⟩=0=⟨(L12L22)(γ1)Lβ(γ2)⟩, and thus, this step has no effect on the lines in question.

It selects only lines which trivially braid with the generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_4^{(1)}$$\end{document}Z4(1) generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2$$\end{document}L12L22. However, we found \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1^2L_2^2$$\end{document}L12L22 exactly by requiring this condition: 60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle {(}L_1^2L_2^2)(\gamma _1)L_\alpha (\gamma _2)\rangle =0=\langle {(}L_1^2L_2^2)(\gamma _1)L_\beta (\gamma _2)\rangle , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨(L12L22)(γ1)Lα(γ2)⟩=0=⟨(L12L22)(γ1)Lβ(γ2)⟩, and thus, this step has no effect on the lines in question.

Noninvertible 1-Form Symmetry

Let us now discuss the fusion rules of the noninvertible electric symmetry. The physical picture is similar to the one we discussed in the previous subsection: the general slogan is that the coefficients of the fusion rules of topological defects are not given by integers but by TQFTs of the same dimension (now two instead of three).

First of all, eventual fusion interfaces will be graded by the invertible part:63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Remark 2

Since the behaviour of line and point defects is completely symmetrical under the exchange:66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L\longleftrightarrow \Phi , \quad \quad (m)\longleftrightarrow (w), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L⟷Φ,(m)⟷(w),we will often discuss only one of the two defects.

The technical proof of the statement relies on the following two lemmas.

Lemma 1

In the theory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_2^{N_1,p_1}\otimes \mathcal {A}_2^{N_2,p_2}$$\end{document}A2N1,p1⊗A2N2,p2 resulting from the fusion of two minimal ones, lines (respectively points) of bulk charge multiple of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{p_3}{N_3}$$\end{document}p3N3 forms a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\mathbb {Z}_L\times \mathbb {Z}_M)/ \mathbb {Z}_{M'}$$\end{document}(ZL×ZM)/ZM′ subgroup generated by:69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_\alpha =L_1L_2; \quad \quad L_\beta =L_1^{(p_1^{-1})_{N_1}K_1}L_2^{-(p_2^{-1})_{N_2}K_2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lα=L1L2;Lβ=L1(p1-1)N1K1L2-(p2-1)N2K2.Moreover, points and lines of different types do not interact with each other:70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [L_\alpha (\gamma ),\Phi _\beta (x)] = 0 = [L_\beta (\gamma ),\Phi _\alpha (x)]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[Lα(γ),Φβ(x)]=0=[Lβ(γ),Φα(x)].

The commutation relation is easily checked. It is also easy to show that:71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha _m(L_\alpha )=\frac{p_3}{N_3}, \quad \operatorname {ord}(L_\alpha )=MK_1K_2=L;\quad \quad \alpha _m(L_\beta )=0, \quad \operatorname {ord}(L_\beta )=M. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αm(Lα)=p3N3,ord(Lα)=MK1K2=L;αm(Lβ)=0,ord(Lβ)=M.Let’s show that there are exactly M decoupled lines (and thus M lines for each fixed coupling). In fact, the magnetic coupling can be seen as a homeomorphism:72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \mathbb {Z}_{N_1}\times \mathbb {Z}_{N_2}&\xrightarrow {\alpha _m}\mathbb {Z}_{L}\\ (u,v)&\rightarrow up_1K_2+vp_2K_1 . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ZN1×ZN2→αmZL(u,v)→up1K2+vp2K1.This morphism is surjective. In fact, we have:73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha _m\left( L_1^{x(p_1^{-1})_{N_1}}L_2^{y(p_2^{-1})_{N_2}}\right) =xK_2+yK_1, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}αmL1x(p1-1)N1L2y(p2-1)N2=xK2+yK1,and, by Bézout’s theorem, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {span}_\mathbb {Z}(K_1,K_2)=\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}spanZ(K1,K2)=Z. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\beta $$\end{document}Lβ is an element of the kernel and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {ord}(L_\beta )=M$$\end{document}ord(Lβ)=M, it generates the whole kernel.

It is also easy to check that lines of the type \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_\alpha ^{i\frac{L}{M'}}$$\end{document}LαiLM′ are decoupled as well.
