We can be even mere precise: since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {ord}(L_\alpha ^{\frac{L}{M'}})=M'$$\end{document}ord(LαLM′)=M′, c has to be of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c=c'\frac{M}{M'}$$\end{document}c=c′MM′ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c'\in \mathbb {Z}_{M'}^\times $$\end{document}c′∈ZM′×. In conclusion, they organize themselves in a group of the form:74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\mathbb {Z}_L\times \mathbb {Z}_M}{\langle (\frac{L}{M'},-c')\rangle } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ZL×ZM⟨(LM′,-c′)⟩where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\frac{L}{M'}, -c')$$\end{document}(LM′,-c′) is the generator of a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{M'}$$\end{document}ZM′ subgroup. We can thus conclude with a counting argument, as we described a subgroup of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _m^{-1}(\mathbb {Z}_{\frac{L}{M'}})$$\end{document}αm-1(ZLM′) which has exactly the right number \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{LM}{M'}$$\end{document}LMM′ of elements.

Lemma 2

In a generic 2-dimensional theory with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_n^{(0)}\times \mathbb {Z}_m^{(1)}$$\end{document}Zn(0)×Zm(1) symmetries15:Gauging a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{n'}^{(0)}\subset \mathbb {Z}_n^{(0)}$$\end{document}Zn′(0)⊂Zn(0) subgroup identifies lines which differ by an element of this subgroup (which, in particular, reduces the symmetry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_n^{(0)}\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}_{\frac{n}{n'}}^{(0)}$$\end{document}Zn(0)→Znn′(0)); and selects the points with charge multiple of n'n′ (the "gauge invariant" ones).16 Moreover there is a new quantum symmetry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\mathbb {Z}}_{n'}^{(0)}$$\end{document}Z^n′(0) whose topological defects are Wilson lines for the gauged \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{n'}^{(0)}$$\end{document}Zn′(0). Gauging a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{m'}^{(1)}\subset \mathbb {Z}_m^{(1)}$$\end{document}Zm′(1)⊂Zm(1) subgroup with discrete torsion \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in H^2(B^2\mathbb {Z}_{m'};U(1))=\mathbb {Z}_{m'}$$\end{document}k∈H2(B2Zm′;U(1))=Zm′ identifies points which differ by an element of this subgroup (which, in particular, reduces the symmetry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_m^{(1)}\rightarrow \mathbb {Z}_{\frac{m}{m'}}^{(1)}$$\end{document}Zm(1)→Zmm′(1)); and selects the lines with charge multiple of m'm′.17

By putting together these lemmas, one can easily show that the interface implementing the half-space gauging of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{M'}^{(0)}\times \mathbb {Z}_{M'}^{(1)}$$\end{document}ZM′(0)×ZM′(1) discussed in Eq. (74) provides a morphism75Here, we have omitted the line operators generating the dual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{M'}$$\end{document}ZM′ symmetry from the right-hand side. This symmetry is trivially acting: since in the original theory there are no twisted sector operators (as an immediate consequence of the fact that all the defects are topological and non-trivially acting), in the gauged one there are no charged operators under the dual symmetry.18 Moreover, the dual symmetry lines are naturally decoupled from the bulk, meaning they are genuine lines of the defect theory, not operators defined only at the intersection of the electric defect with some bulk surface defect.19

Moreover, since the theory \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {A}_2^{\frac{M}{M'}}$$\end{document}A2MM′ is not simple, one can further project down to one of its components by gauging its \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_{\frac{M}{M'}}^{(1)}$$\end{document}ZMM′(1) symmetry. The desired morphism is given by the composition of the two: which actually corresponds to gauging the full \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}_M^{(1)}$$\end{document}ZM(1) from the beginning.20

Remark 3

It is important to note that, because of the bulk coupling, a general operator built from lines and points of the minimal theory is technically a junction between the theory itself and the bulk symmetry defects to which the theory is coupled. In the case under investigation, however, we explicitly dealt with decoupled defects, i.e. proper (higher) endomorphisms.

Associators

In order to fully specify the symmetry category \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C}$$\end{document}C, one has to provide the data of the associativity of the fusion operation ⊗ ⊗.

Noninvertible 0-Form Symmetry

Let’s study the F-symbol of the shift symmetry, which amounts to a review of [48, section 3.4] by analogy. In order to understand the theory of the F-symbol, we need to analyse the interfaces it is made of. Being gauging interfaces of lines in 3-dimensional defects, they serve multiple roles (see section B):they identify lines which differ by elements of the gauged algebra;they constrain to the interface the lines which braid non-trivially with the algebra object;they allow the gauged lines to end topologically on the interface.The F-symbol bubble has gauging interfaces on both sides: on the left-hand side the successive gauge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}_{23}$$\end{document}A23 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}_{1(23)}$$\end{document}A1(23) leads to gauge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}^-=\mathbb {A}_{23}\rhd \mathbb {A}_{1(23)}$$\end{document}A-=A23⊳A1(23); on the right-hand side the successive gauge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}_{12)}$$\end{document}A12) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}_{(12)3}$$\end{document}A(12)3 leads to gauge of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}^+=\mathbb {A}_{12}\rhd \mathbb {A}_{(12)3}$$\end{document}A+=A12⊳A(12)3. The F-symbol bubble can be compressed along the transversal direction, leading to a setup similar to that of the SymTFT. The actual F-symbol is obtained by shrinking along the slab direction: topological lines endable on both boundaries give birth to topological point operators, and their braiding with the other lines of the theory descends to a linking. Noninvertible 1-form symmetry

Let’s now study the F-symbol of the electric symmetry. There is a strong analogy to that of the shift symmetry or the axial symmetry of [48], but there are important differences. The F-symbol is now a one-dimensional topological quantum mechanics rather than a 2d TQFT, and the bulk contains both line and point operators that descend to the interface.

In this case, the F-symbol is composed of gauging interfaces of both lines and points, which implement the following operations:they identify lines and points which differ by elements of the gauged algebras;they constrain to the interface the lines which link non-trivially with the point algebra object, and vice versa the point non-trivially charged under the algebra of lines;they allow the gauged lines to end topologically on the interface.The F-symbol bubble has gauging interfaces on both sides: on the left-hand side the successive gauging of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} Detecting the F-symbol

Sweeping a shift F-symbol bubble through a ’t Hooft line \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_m$$\end{document}Hm attaches it to a magnetic defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{\frac{mp}{N}}$$\end{document}ηmpN(m) which itself attaches to lines on the bubble (we get three lines one on each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{\frac{p_i}{N_i}}$$\end{document}DpiNi(s) component). The difference is that now these lines are fully inside the bubble, and therefore shrinking they fuse into a line of the F-symbol TFT. Moreover, choosing m=Nm=N, the magnetic defect is trivial (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{\frac{Np}{N}} \sim 1$$\end{document}ηNpN(m)∼1) and shrinking the bubble will then lead to a genuine deconfined line of the theory of the F-symbol.

Axion strings transverse to the electric F-symbol: This case is precisely analogous to that of ’t Hooft lines and the shift F-symbol. Sweeping an electric bubble through an axion string \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_w$$\end{document}Sw again attaches it to a magnetic defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{\frac{mp}{N} }$$\end{document}ηmpN(m) which itself attaches to lines on the bubble. Choosing w=Nw=N the string doesn’t get attached to any magnetic defect and the nontrivial configuration is localized only on the F-symbol. Using the result Eq. (89) we obtain: 95\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boldsymbol{F}^{(e)}[\tilde{\mathbb {A}}^+\pi ^+;\tilde{\mathbb {A}}^-\pi ^-]_{\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p_1/N_1},\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p_2/N_2},\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p_3/N_3}}\otimes S_N=S_N\otimes d_{0}\bigoplus _{q=0}^{J-1}e^{\frac{2\pi iq}{J}} \eta ^{(w)}_{\frac{q}{N_{123}}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F(e)[A~+π+;A~-π-]Dp1/N1(e),Dp2/N2(e),Dp3/N3(e)⊗SN=SN⊗d0⨁q=0J-1e2πiqJηqN123(w), and shrinking the (now one-dimensional) support of the F-symbol again leaves behind the factor 96\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} d_{0}\sum _{q=0}^{J-1}e^{\frac{2\pi iq}{J}} = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} d_0 & J = 1 \\ 0 & J > 1, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}d0∑q=0J-1e2πiqJ=d0J=10J>1, so that an axion string distinguishes between a trivial (J = 1J=1) and nontrivial (J > 1J>1) F-symbol. Again, the axion strings with w = Nw=N are exactly those unaffected by the non-invertible Gauss law [72].

Axion strings transverse to the electric F-symbol: This case is precisely analogous to that of ’t Hooft lines and the shift F-symbol. Constraints and dualities

Interplay with the (non-invertible) higher group structure In this section, we are going to explore how the nontrivial higher group structure affects associativity.We remember that the one-dimensional intersection of an electric defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e) and a shift defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{p'/N'}$$\end{document}Dp′/N′(s) sources a magnetic defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{(pp')/(NN')}$$\end{document}η(pp′)/(NN′)(m). If we move this configuration across an F-symbol for the shift defect, the sourced magnetic defect will split and recompose according to the shift F-symbol bubble (see Fig. 15). After shrinking, we will end up with a magnetic F-symbol. However, since the F-symbol of the magnetic symmetry is trivial, this part of the higher group doesn’t lead to new constraints. Similarly, moving the configuration across the electric F-symbol will have the same effect.Similarly, recall that the zero-dimensional intersection of two electric defects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p'/N'}$$\end{document}Dp′/N′(e) sources a winding defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(w)}_{(pp')/(NN')}$$\end{document}η(pp′)/(NN′)(w). Moving this configuration across an electric F-symbol gives rise to a winding F-symbol bubble (see Fig. 15), which again is trivial and does not lead to new constraints.Fig. 15The presence of a (non-invertible) higher group structure implies that moving an electric symmetry defect through an F-symbol of the shift symmetry, produces a F-symbol of the magnetic symmetry (which is trivial in our case)

We remember that the one-dimensional intersection of an electric defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e) and a shift defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{p'/N'}$$\end{document}Dp′/N′(s) sources a magnetic defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{(pp')/(NN')}$$\end{document}η(pp′)/(NN′)(m). If we move this configuration across an F-symbol for the shift defect, the sourced magnetic defect will split and recompose according to the shift F-symbol bubble (see Fig. 15). After shrinking, we will end up with a magnetic F-symbol. However, since the F-symbol of the magnetic symmetry is trivial, this part of the higher group doesn’t lead to new constraints. Similarly, moving the configuration across the electric F-symbol will have the same effect.

We remember that the one-dimensional intersection of an electric defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e) and a shift defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(s)}_{p'/N'}$$\end{document}Dp′/N′(s) sources a magnetic defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(m)}_{(pp')/(NN')}$$\end{document}η(pp′)/(NN′)(m). If we move this configuration across an F-symbol for the shift defect, the sourced magnetic defect will split and recompose according to the shift F-symbol bubble (see Fig. 15). After shrinking, we will end up with a magnetic F-symbol. However, since the F-symbol of the magnetic symmetry is trivial, this part of the higher group doesn’t lead to new constraints. Similarly, moving the configuration across the electric F-symbol will have the same effect.

Similarly, recall that the zero-dimensional intersection of two electric defects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p'/N'}$$\end{document}Dp′/N′(e) sources a winding defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(w)}_{(pp')/(NN')}$$\end{document}η(pp′)/(NN′)(w). Moving this configuration across an electric F-symbol gives rise to a winding F-symbol bubble (see Fig. 15), which again is trivial and does not lead to new constraints.

Similarly, recall that the zero-dimensional intersection of two electric defects \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p/N}$$\end{document}Dp/N(e) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{p'/N'}$$\end{document}Dp′/N′(e) sources a winding defect \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta ^{(w)}_{(pp')/(NN')}$$\end{document}η(pp′)/(NN′)(w). Moving this configuration across an electric F-symbol gives rise to a winding F-symbol bubble (see Fig. 15), which again is trivial and does not lead to new constraints.

The presence of a (non-invertible) higher group structure implies that moving an electric symmetry defect through an F-symbol of the shift symmetry, produces a F-symbol of the magnetic symmetry (which is trivial in our case)

A similar argument also applies to the shift symmetry. Example

We conclude the section with an explicit computation of an electric F-symbol, and the check of the corresponding duality. We are going to explicitly compute the F-symbol for the following configuration:102\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {1}\xrightarrow {\quad \overline{m}_{4,4 } \quad } \mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{1/4} \otimes \mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{1/4} \otimes \mathcal {D}^{(e)}_{1/2} \xrightarrow {\quad m_{2,2}\circ m_{2,4} \quad } \;\mathbb {1}.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}1→m¯4,4D1/4(e)⊗D1/4(e)⊗D1/2(e)→m2,2∘m2,41.as well as verify the proposed duality 99.
